\subsection{Empirically Optimal Weights}

Using linear regression on the per-ply positional data from all games, the following weights best predict game outcomes (win=1, draw=0.5, loss=0):

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_{opt} = 0.00 \Delta S + 0.00 \Delta M + 0.00 \Delta K + 1.00 \Delta T'
\end{equation}

Weights: $w_S = 0.001$, $w_M = 0.001$, $w_K = 0.001$, $w_T = 0.998$ ($R^2 = 0.000$)

\section{Interpretation}

These predictions tracked the eventual winner in each decisive game. 
Three prediction algorithms are compared:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Per-ply}: Predicts WIN if FTI $> \epsilon$ for 10 consecutive plies (raw values)
\item \textbf{Windowed}: Same logic but applied to a 10-ply rolling average
\item \textbf{Balance}: Uses normalized balance $B_n$ (plies ahead $-$ plies behind) and volatility $V$ (sign changes). Predicts DRAW if $|B_n| < 0.2$ or $V \ge 10$
\end{itemize}

The Balance algorithm explicitly models draws by detecting games where neither side maintains a consistent advantage, or where the advantage changes hands frequently.

The different FTI signatures may perform better or worse depending on the playing style of the players being analyzed. FTI2 (Tactical) tends to predict well for players who emphasize territorial control and tactical threats, while FTI3 (Strategic) may better capture the style of players who prioritize space and piece activity.

\appendix
\chapter{Methodology: How Metrics Are Computed}

This appendix explains how the positional metrics are computed.


This section explains how Stockfish's classical evaluation computes each positional factor. Note that modern Stockfish primarily uses NNUE (neural network) evaluation, but the classical evaluation terms provide interpretable metrics for educational purposes.

\section{Space}

Space evaluation counts squares in the center (files c-f, ranks 2-4 for White, 5-7 for Black) that are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Attacked by at least one friendly pawn, AND
\item Not occupied by any enemy piece
\end{enumerate}

The computation is weighted by the number of pieces behind the pawn chain---this rewards positions where you control territory AND have pieces to exploit that space. The space term is scaled by total piece count; it matters more in closed positions with many pieces.

\section{Mobility}

Mobility counts legal moves for each piece type, with different weights:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Knights}: Safe squares attacked (excluding squares defended by enemy pawns)
\item \textbf{Bishops}: Safe squares on diagonals (bonus for long, unobstructed diagonals)
\item \textbf{Rooks}: Squares on files/ranks (bonus for open files, 7th rank penetration)
\item \textbf{Queens}: Combination of bishop and rook mobility patterns
\end{itemize}

``Safe squares" exclude those defended by enemy pawns or where exchanges would lose material. Mobility in the opponent's territory is valued higher than mobility in your own.

\section{King Safety}

King safety combines several factors:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pawn shield}: Pawns on 2nd/3rd rank directly in front of the castled king
\item \textbf{King tropism}: Distance of enemy pieces to your king (closer = more dangerous)
\item \textbf{Attack units}: Count of enemy pieces attacking squares adjacent to your king
\item \textbf{Safe checks}: Number of safe checking squares available to the opponent
\end{itemize}

\section{Threats}

Threat evaluation considers:
\begin{itemize}
\item Hanging pieces (attacked but not adequately defended)
\item Safe pawn advances that create threats
\item Minor pieces attacking major pieces
\item Weak squares in the pawn structure
\end{itemize}

\section{Middlegame vs Endgame (MG/EG)}

Each term has separate middlegame (MG) and endgame (EG) values. Stockfish blends these based on remaining material (the ``phase"). With all pieces on the board, MG dominates; as pieces trade, EG values become more important. For example, space matters greatly in the middlegame but is nearly irrelevant in endgames.

\section{Game Character Classification}

Games are classified based on evaluation volatility and directionality. Let $m_2$ be the maximum evaluation for White during the game (in pawns) and $m_1$ be the minimum. The \emph{spread} $d = m_2 - m_1$ measures the total evaluation range.

\paragraph{Spread Classifications.} Based on the magnitude of $d$:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Balanced} ($d < 1$): Minimal advantage shifts; controlled, technical play with neither side gaining a significant edge.
\item \textbf{Tense} ($1 \le d < 3$): Normal competitive tension; typical of well-played games where small advantages are contested.
\item \textbf{Tactical} ($3 \le d < 6$): Significant evaluation swings; tactical complications, missed opportunities, or speculative play.
\item \textbf{Chaotic} ($d \ge 6$): Wild swings in evaluation; likely includes major blunders, speculative sacrifices, or complex tactical melees.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Directionality Classifications.} Based on whether the advantage changed hands:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{One-sided}: Either $m_1 > -\frac{1}{2}$ (White always comfortable) or $m_2 < \frac{1}{2}$ (Black always comfortable). The leading side maintained control throughout.
\item \textbf{Seesaw}: Both $m_1 < -1$ and $m_2 > 1$. The advantage genuinely changed hands, with each side holding a significant advantage at some point.
\item \textbf{Normal}: Neither one-sided nor seesaw; moderate swings without complete reversals.
\end{itemize}

These classifications combine to describe the overall game character. For example, a ``tactical seesaw battle" indicates significant evaluation swings ($3 \le d < 6$) with the advantage changing hands multiple times.

\section{Fireteam Index (FTI) and Win Prediction}

The Fireteam Index is a composite metric inspired by the ancient mathematical board game \textit{Rithmomachia}, where a ``progression victory'' is achieved when a coordinated group of pieces (a ``fireteam'') establishes control deep in enemy territory. Similarly, in chess, sustained positional dominance across multiple factors often precedes victory.

The FTI weights can be thought of as \emph{signatures} that capture different playing styles. By adjusting which positional factors receive emphasis, the FTI can be tuned to better predict outcomes for players with different strategic preferences.

\paragraph{Threats Scaling.} The raw Threats metric ($T$) produces values typically ranging from 5--20, while Space, Mobility, and King Safety range from approximately 0.1--0.5. To make these comparable, Threats is divided by a scaling factor of 20 before weighting. The scaled value $T' = T/20$ brings Threats into a similar range as the other metrics.

\paragraph{FTI1: Harmonious Formula.} The harmonious Fireteam Index weights all four positional metrics equally (with Threats pre-scaled):

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_1 = \frac{1}{4} \Delta S + \frac{1}{4} \Delta M + \frac{1}{4} \Delta K + \frac{1}{4} \Delta T'
\end{equation}

where $\Delta S$ = Space, $\Delta M$ = Mobility, $\Delta K$ = King Safety, and $\Delta T' = T/20$ = scaled Threats, each computed as (Player's value $-$ Opponent's value). Positive FTI indicates the player has overall positional superiority; negative indicates the opponent is better.

\paragraph{FTI2: Tactical.} This variant emphasizes territorial control and tactical pressure through Space and Threats:

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_2 = 0.6 \Delta S + 0.1 \Delta M + 0.3 \Delta T'
\end{equation}

\paragraph{FTI3: Strategic.} This variant emphasizes long-term spatial dominance:

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_3 = 0.7 \Delta S + 0.1 \Delta M + 0.1 \Delta K + 0.1 \Delta T'
\end{equation}

\paragraph{FTI4: Dynamic.} This variant emphasizes piece activity and maneuvering:

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_4 = 0.2 \Delta S + 0.6 \Delta M + 0.2 \Delta T'
\end{equation}

\paragraph{FTI5: Solid.} This variant emphasizes king safety and defensive play:

\begin{equation}
\text{FTI}_5 = 0.3 \Delta S + 0.2 \Delta M + 0.5 \Delta K
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Prediction Algorithms.} Three algorithms are used to predict game outcomes based on FTI (applied independently to each of the five variants):

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Per-Ply (Raw)}: Examines the raw FTI value at each ply after the opening phase (ply 16, approximately 8 moves). If FTI $> \epsilon$ (default $\epsilon = 0.25$) for 10 consecutive plies (5 full moves), a WIN is predicted for that player.

\item \textbf{Windowed (Smoothed)}: Computes a 10-ply rolling average of FTI before applying the same streak detection. This smooths out tactical noise and captures sustained trends rather than momentary fluctuations.

\item \textbf{Balance (Confidence-based)}: Predicts outcomes using a confidence score that combines balance and volatility:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Normalized Balance} $B_n = \frac{(\text{plies with FTI} > \epsilon) - (\text{plies with FTI} < -\epsilon)}{\text{total plies}}$
\item \emph{Volatility} $V$ = number of times FTI crosses zero (sign changes)
\item \emph{Volatility threshold} $V_{\max} = 0.2 \times \text{total plies}$ (scales with game length)
\item \emph{Confidence} $C = |B_n| \times \max(0, 1 - V/V_{\max})$
\end{itemize}
The prediction is: WIN if $C > 0.15$ and $B_n > 0$; LOSS if $C > 0.15$ and $B_n < 0$; DRAW otherwise. Low confidence (due to balanced play or high volatility) predicts a draw.
\end{enumerate}

The Per-Ply and Windowed algorithms predict DRAW only when no streak is achieved, whereas the Balance algorithm explicitly detects drawish characteristics (balanced plies, back-and-forth play).

\paragraph{Interpretation.} The FTI prediction measures \emph{positional} dominance, which is a necessary but not sufficient condition for winning. A player may dominate positionally yet fail to convert due to:
\begin{itemize}
\item Tactical errors (blunders, missed combinations)
\item Time pressure in rapid/blitz games
\item Tenacious defense by the opponent
\item Drawing tendencies in certain structures (opposite-colored bishops, fortresses)
\end{itemize}

Conversely, a player may win without ever achieving sustained FTI dominance through a single tactical strike or opponent error. The FTI metric is most predictive in games where positional understanding---rather than pure calculation---determines the outcome.

\paragraph{Choosing a Signature.} Different FTI signatures may perform better for different players or playing styles. FTI2 (Tactical) tends to predict well for players who build up territorial control and convert through direct threats. FTI3 (Strategic) may better capture the style of players who emphasize piece activity and spatial domination. Comparing prediction accuracy across signatures can reveal which positional factors most strongly correlate with a player's wins.

\paragraph{For Draws.} When analyzing drawn games, the FTI is computed from both players' perspectives. This reveals whether:
\begin{itemize}
\item Neither side achieved dominance (a ``true draw'' with balanced play)
\item One side dominated but failed to convert (a ``missed win'')
\item Both sides dominated at different points (a ``mutual chances draw'')
\end{itemize}

\end{document}
